- WHAT: A proactive coding-agent system for one GitHub repo, packaged for DeepSeek Harness: listens to GitHub (and optionally Feishu); when an allowed person asks for work, it opens a work item, executes it in the agent's own session/directory, and answers in the original thread (e.g., with a PR). Architecture: one router session, one isolated worker per ticket, ticket lifecycle with time limits and accept modes, a web panel surfacing only what needs a person. Stated principle: act only when asked or when a team-written rule allows; speak only when someone must act or can use a result. README's own warnings: "Research prototype for reference only, not an official DeepSeek product… Treat it as research code, not production-ready software"; the published code is adapted from — not identical to — the internal system the paper describes.
- SYSTEM: Fleet infrastructure as DESIGN REFERENCE (router/worker-per-ticket isolation, time-limited tickets with accept modes, attention-minimization doctrine). No run test. **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY.**

### B3 — @AnnatarXBT — "The End of Harness Engineering" (method)
- Post: https://x.com/AnnatarXBT/status/2116494643739173149, quoting @Av1dlive's builder's guide "How to Build Agentic Harness using Jev."
- WHAT: A paper summary with a sharp thesis: hand-writing the harness around a frozen model is a depreciating skill because a person-written harness is one unscored guess; a loop-written harness grades itself — a small model (4B, per the post) reads what failed, proposes 8 edits, and only the edit scoring best on held-out questions survives, while the big model stays frozen. The human's job collapses to one artifact: the scoring set. One-liner: "write the exam before the code." The paper itself is NOT resolved this run (no arXiv ID, DOI, or link captured; title page + Fig. 1 in the post image, values not transcribed) — everything above is the poster's summary, UNVERIFIED at the source.
- SYSTEM: Fleet infrastructure + GSE judge/eval lane — an independent restatement of the corpus's Laya-audition rule (the exam — frozen set + validated scorer + pre-registered kill bars — written before the candidate ran). **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY** (source resolution owed: one search for the paper's canonical record).

### B4 — @BOT23749 quoting @sfdata9ers — Depth-adjusted catchable % (QB Catchability) (FOOTBALL)
- Post: https://x.com/BOT23749/status/2116675893838115145, quoting @sfdata9ers' "QB Passing Profile through Week 7" (Fantasy Points Data). Chart values transcribed by the browser pass at screenshot resolution — best-effort, flagged inline by the pass itself.
- WHAT (construction printed on the chart): for each QB, catchable-ball rate is computed separately by target depth (behind LOS / 0–9 / 10–19 / 20+ yards), then re-weighted by a COMMON depth distribution (17.4% / 48.9% / 22.2% / 11.1%) so every QB is graded on the same throw mix — "Adjusted score = each depth's catchable rate × the common weight, summed." Min 105 attempts, Weeks 1–7. Leaders per the transcription: Caleb Williams 88.7 (#1), Drake Maye 87.9, Josh Allen ≈86.8–87.3 (#3 — read two ways), Geno Smith 86.7, Sam Darnold 86.5, Joe Burrow 85.8, Jared Goff 85.5; Patrick Mahomes 82.9 ("ranks 26th in accuracy" per the post text); Jalen Hurts 75.9 and Daniel Jones 75.7 at the foot. The chart itself warns: "Rounded source inputs make ranks approximate." This is the catchable-ball family from @ghatz_28's CTBL% dossier (Oct 29), now with explicit standardization.
- USE/LAYER: Matchups layer (QB accuracy vs coverage). GSE cannot compute catchable % in-house (charted class) but CAN adopt the standardization pattern for any mix-contaminated stat it does compute (e.g., depth-adjusted completion % from pbp air-yards buckets, labeled a proxy). Pairs with W4's Air Yards Lost to Drops: both halves of the QB-accuracy ledger, both depth-aware, sourced the same day.
- RISKS: Transcription-grade values; charter variance; common weights erase real scheme information along with noise. **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY** (standardization pattern ADOPT-worthy for proxy stats).

### B5 — @rufusjonz — Balanced QB "nuke" exposure: a DraftKings GPP game plan (FOOTBALL / DFS)
- Post: https://x.com/rufusjonz/status/2116613868508049612 (chart values transcribed in full by the browser pass). Author: @rufusjonz (MobyWhaler); projections are the author's (UNVERIFIED); salaries are DraftKings'.
- WHAT: A portfolio-construction method for DFS GPPs in one chart: every QB on the Week 8 DK slate (salary $7,700 Josh Allen down to $4,900 Malik Willis), a target exposure split (BALANCED vs HEAVY columns), two ceiling projections (labeled 26 CEIL / 25 CEIL), and the salary multiples a QB must hit (3X–6X value thresholds). The week's thesis, stated in the post: contrary to the author's usual cheap-QB approach, 1–2 of the higher-priced QBs project to "nuke" for 35+, in a week "without massive chalk" — so the plan concentrates 65% of exposure in five top QBs (Allen 15%, Burrow 10%, Lamar Jackson 10%, Maye 15%, Trevor Lawrence 15% balanced) with a mid-tier band (Goff/Stafford/Dak Prescott/Mahomes) sharing most of the rest, 5% max caps on the fragile names (Kyler Murray, Michael Penix Jr.), and Deshaun Watson ($5,400) flagged "ALL" with a 0.0 in one ceiling column — the chart's own internal contradiction, recorded as printed. Geno Smith appears at $5,200 with no team named — no ruling conflict.
- USE/LAYER: GSE's DFS package surface (/fantasy/dfs) — as construction grammar, not plays: exposure budgeting by tier, ceiling columns beside salary, value multiples as shared language; the "no massive chalk" week-type call is a slate-classification step (construction follows slate type). Nothing in the chart is republished as a GSE play. **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY.**

### B6 — @Stokastic — Ask the Sims: Tampa's passing tree with Egbuka out (FOOTBALL)
- Post: https://x.com/Stokastic/status/2116523084786122834 (100,000 sims of Falcons–Buccaneers; grid values partially legible, headline figures in text). Vendor sim percentages UNVERIFIED.
- WHAT: A simulation-distribution product: 100,000 simulated games produce, per Tampa receiver, the share of sims leading the team in receiving yards, target share, and the distribution of catch yardage. Headlines: Chris Godwin Jr. soaks up almost a quarter of the targets; Ted Hurst III is the deep shot (22% of his catches gain 20+ yards, the highest rate in the sims). The absence premise ("Emeka Egbuka out Sunday") is the poster's claim — DISPUTED in-corpus (see Part 4): concussion-clearance items for Egbuka circulated the same day and the official designation governs. (Corpus note carried in the source dossier: the Godwin case was separately flagged contaminated in the Argon file on identity grounds; this dossier records the sims post as a cited vendor claim and takes no position from it.)
- USE/LAYER: Usage layer method note — when a WR1 exits, the honest output is a reallocation DISTRIBUTION, not a single "next man up" line; the Hurst callout format ("X owns the 20+ yard tail") is a shape-of-distribution claim that pairs with props reasoning better than a projection point.
- RISKS: Premise disputed; vendor sims with unpublished inputs; a 100,000-sim count is precision theater if input distributions are wrong. TEST: settle the premise against the official designation record before any use of the reallocation shape for this game. **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY.**

### B7 — @tangotiger — RE288 by venue+season (method transfer)
- Post: https://x.com/tangotiger/status/2116622443137671277 → tangotiger.com blog (text read from the post's screenshots). Source: Tom Tango, the metric's maintainer for Savant — a primary source by construction.
- WHAT: Baseball's RE24 (expected runs by base-out state, Palmer/Thorn, ~45 years old) expanded to RE288 — 24 base-out states × 12 ball-strike counts — now computed per venue+season, shipping on Savant. Two design decisions carry the lesson: the chart is a "semi-Markov process," not pure empirics, because small cells (rare base-count states) cannot support raw frequencies; and the environment adjustment is applied to the expectancy surface itself, not as a post-hoc park factor.
- SYSTEM: GSE prediction engine, as the baseball mirror of two live problems: (1) sparse-cell expectancy (down/distance/score-state WP and EP surfaces have the same thin-cell disease RE288 solves with a semi-Markov blend); (2) environment-first adjustment (venue/weather effects inside the expectancy surface, not bolted onto outputs). Transfer is methodological, not data. **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY.**

### AM-01 — @simplifyinai — 7-free-utilities carousel — LEARN-ONLY
- **What it is:** Listicle carousel naming seven free utilities: a Linux GPU unlock for RTX cards, a Windows 11 debloat script, Claude Code used to declutter an Android TV home screen, an ad-free YouTube TV client, an Android debloater, an app patcher, network-wide ad blocking. The GPU item claims a 1.8x BF16 speedup (repo's own claim, UNVERIFIED).
- **Where:** Post: https://www.instagram.com/p/DfKB_shGSaT/ — Verified primaries: https://github.com/Zagitta/gpu-unlock (13,686★, no license detected, pushed 2026-10-29; promises up to 2× tensor throughput on 20/30/40-series cards) and https://github.com/Raphire/Win11Debloat (61,348★, MIT, pushed 2026-10-30; PowerShell script removing preinstalled apps, disabling telemetry/features). Remaining five (SmartTube, Universal Android Debloater, ReVanced Manager, Pi-hole) well-known but not individually re-verified this run.
- **How to use:** Win11Debloat: run the PowerShell script with defaults or pick removals. gpu-unlock: Linux kernel-level module for Ampere/Ada cards — a driver-layer change, not an app.
- **System:** Windows host (fleet's other seat) for Win11Debloat — same job as the Oct 30 RemoveMacAI find, on Garrett's actual platform. gpu-unlock fits the Kaggle/local-GPU inference lane in principle only — requires a Linux box with a 20/30/40-series card, not current verified hardware.
- **Risks:** gpu-unlock modifies kernel/driver behavior (can break a driver stack); license unconfirmed; speedup figures are the repo's own claims, UNVERIFIED. Debloat scripts can remove components other software expects. The carousel's framing conflates the tools with Claude — they are independent of Claude.
- **Verdict:** LEARN-ONLY. Test path: Win11Debloat on a snapshotted non-critical Windows profile; gpu-unlock only if a sacrificial Linux GPU box exists.

### AM-07 — @gittrend.io — SamSUCKS (Plex client for Samsung TVs) — WATCH
- **What it is:** Screen recording walking the README of SamSUCKS, a from-scratch Plex client for Samsung TVs built for speed. Narration contrasts the official app's slow open with roughly one-second opens, ~50 fps scrolling, and direct-play of nearly the whole library without transcoding — those performance figures are the repo's own, UNVERIFIED.
- **Where:** https://github.com/rfaile313/samsucks (verified: 99★, GPL-3.0). Post: https://www.instagram.com/p/DfJ7UydDUIw/
- **How to use:** Per README: clone, run setup (installs Samsung's TV CLI tools, ~270 MB, finds the TV on the network), sideload to a 2020-or-newer Samsung TV in Developer Mode; plain JavaScript, no build step; needs a Plex Media Server on the same network.
- **System:** Household stack (personal, per nothing-gets-killed rule) — replaces a slow official client at zero cost if Garrett's TV is Samsung. Transferable pattern: "official client is slow; single-purpose rewrite beats it" — same wedge as several of his own lanes; its README comparison-table style is a clean way to sell a speed fix.
- **Risks:** Small single-maintainer project; Developer Mode required; GPL-3.0 (fine for personal use); self-measured performance claims.
- **Verdict:** WATCH — test only if a Samsung TV is actually in the house (sideload, time the open vs the official app).

### AM-10 — @technicallyhash — Claude Code as 20 parts (infographic) — LEARN-ONLY
- **What it is:** Static infographic reel teaching Claude Code as 20 named parts in learning order: what loads before you type (project rules file, skills, MCP, settings); how a session works (tools, context window, compaction, prompt caching); control (permissions, plan mode, checkpointing, sandboxing); reach (subagents, hooks, worktrees, background bash); tuning (commands, output styles, extended thinking, non-interactive mode). A "WORDS" comment gate fronts the creator's free plain-English guide (not engaged).
- **Where:** https://www.instagram.com/p/De6HvWuEq5m/ — the product is Anthropic's Claude Code; the map itself is fully in the caption.
- **How to use:** As an audit checklist for each of Garrett's Claude surfaces (claude-ds harness, Claude Code sessions, web lane); settings, hooks, and checkpointing are the three most commonly skipped parts.
- **System:** Agent fleet infrastructure directly — the DeepSeek-routed claude-ds harness and every Claude Code session the fleet runs. Row order (load → session → control → reach → tune) is a sane configuration-audit sequence for a new seat. Creative angle: turn the 20 parts into a one-page per-seat fleet checklist; pairs with AM-20's full course.
- **Risks:** Creator simplification — a few definitions compressed to looseness (e.g., context-window size stated flatly); gated guide unverified. A study aid, not documentation.
- **Verdict:** LEARN-ONLY.

### AM-14 — @technicallyhash — /awwwards skill — TEST ⭐
- **What it is:** A free MIT agent skill: give it a one-line description of yourself or a project and it designs a website — generates three concepts, keeps the strongest, fixes a color and type system, uses motion only where it helps — then grades its own work: builds the site, screenshots desktop and mobile, checks performance/accessibility measures, iterates until every category reaches 8/10 by its own scoring.
- **Where:** https://github.com/juliobsz/skills (verified: 1,041★, 45 forks, MIT, pushed 2026-10-26, created 2026-10-12; repo also carries a companion /jury skill). Post: https://www.instagram.com/p/DfKRJPUiD5a/
- **How to use:** Per README: install through the skills CLI via npx (`npx skills add`-style flow), which auto-detects the host agent (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, others); invoke with a one-line brief; remove with the same CLI. Skill is free; the host agent may need a paid plan.
- **System:** Kit/client work at the production step — Kit sells one-page websites for $350; this is a one-line-to-finished-site builder with a built-in screenshot QA loop, the exact shape of Kit's preview-page generation (whose QA Kit currently does by hand). Secondarily the AI Marketing OS and GSE's own /packages storefront polish. Creative angles: (a) the self-grading loop (build → screenshot → score → iterate) is the transferable asset even if designs disappoint — it belongs on GSE pages and Kit previews regardless of whose skill runs it; (b) pair with AM-18: agency designer/marketer agents write the brief, /awwwards builds, /jury scores — a full Kit pipeline from three free repos found in one morning; (c) three-concepts-keep-one is a cheap A/B generator for Kit preview variants.
- **Risks:** Young repo (~1K★, created Oct 12); the 8/10 is self-scored — output still needs a human design eye before a client sees it; "award-caliber" is the repo's own framing, UNVERIFIED until a real build is inspected; host-agent plan costs apply.
- **Verdict:** TEST — sweep's #1 test candidate for Kit. Run it on one real Kit prospect brief; inspect output against a hand-built preview for design quality and factual accuracy; time both.

### AM-15 — @gittrend.io — Bang Motion — TEST
- **What it is:** An agent skill that turns a coding agent into a motion designer — openers, promos, product demos, kinetic typography, bumpers, explainers — rendered as a single self-playing index.html rather than a slide deck, with the look derived from the user's brand via style directions and opener concepts.
- **Where:** https://github.com/bangtutorial/bang-motion (verified: 828★, 124 forks, MIT, pushed 2026-09-15, created 2026-09-05). Post: https://www.instagram.com/p/DfKJNWvjPgg/
- **How to use:** Per README: install as a plugin (`/plugin install bang-motion@bang-motion`) or clone into the agent's skills location; works with Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Cursor; prompt for a piece and it produces one HTML file that plays and loops on open.
- **System:** GSE Content OS, wrapper lane only — openers, bumpers, stat-card motion, explainer layers around real game footage. The standing video rule fences this: code-made motion graphics are wrappers/explainers, never a substitute for real footage. Completes a three-piece free wrapper stack: mg-styles-15 (Oct 30) + AM-16's prompt library + Bang Motion — prompt → style → render, all MIT. Single-file HTML output is trivially hostable on the GSE site with no video pipeline.
- **Risks:** Young, small repo; output quality unproven until a render is inspected; HTML motion is not video — platform upload still needs a capture step for Reels/TikTok; gallery examples are the repo's own best cases.
- **Verdict:** TEST — one GSE stat-card opener from an existing template brief; compare against the current opener.

Creative angle: mine by category for GSE shapes (stat cards, matchup explainers, package promos) and build a short internal index — 20 pre-picked GSE-shaped prompts are a tool; 703 unsorted are a haystack.

### AM-17 — @simplifyinai — DeFM (depth-only vision foundation model) — LEARN-ONLY
- **What it is:** DeFM, a depth-only vision foundation model: a backbone trained on 60M depth images by self-distillation so robots can navigate, grasp, and walk from depth alone across different sensors; distilled variants down to a 3M-parameter model; an 8.7ms Jetson figure appears in the README walkthrough.
- **Where:** https://github.com/leggedrobotics/defm (verified: 269★, Apache-2.0; org is ETH Zurich's Robotic Systems Lab). Project page via the repo. Post: https://www.instagram.com/p/DfJUNrZmbK1/
- **How to use:** Per README: `pip install defm`, load via TorchHub (deps: torch, torchvision, numpy, huggingface_hub, omegaconf), or clone for notebooks; pretrained weights published.
- **System:** No current Garrett system runs robots — cataloged for the research corpus and a plausible future project. Real adjacency: GSE's tracking lane reasons about player movement from video; depth-first perception (geometry without appearance) is the same intellectual move as tracking-first reasoning (position without narrative). Also: a depth backbone is privacy-light sensing (no faces/identity) — Family Shield-compatible; its distillation ladder (large teacher → 3M-param edge model) is the deployment pattern his local-model lanes keep needing.
- **Risks:** Robotics hardware required for any live use; benchmark figures are the project's own; no GSE application exists today and none should be forced.
- **Verdict:** LEARN-ONLY — read the paper/repo into the research corpus.

### AM-18 — @technicallyhash — The Agency / agency-agents — TEST ⭐
- **What it is:** A free MIT repo holding 283 specialist agent definitions across 18 teams (engineering 66, marketing 37, design 10, sales 9, support 6, plus security, testing, product, finance, game-dev, more). Each agent is a markdown file a coding agent loads as a subagent, carrying its own mission, rules, and step-by-step workflow. An orchestrator file runs a PM → UX-architect → developer/QA loop (up to 3 tries per task, then escalation).
- **Where:** https://github.com/msitarzewski/agency-agents (verified: 165,613★, 26,804 forks, MIT, pushed 2026-10-25, created 2025-10-13; caption's star/fork claims match). Post: https://www.instagram.com/p/DfJ_-rcAqFe/
- **How to use:** Per caption and README: clone, run `./scripts/install.sh --tool claude-code` (installer also targets antigravity, gemini-cli, opencode, others); agents land in the host's agents directory; a division flag installs one team only; then ask the host agent to use a named specialist.
- **System:** Agent fleet infrastructure at the role-definition layer — Garrett's fleet (Motif, Hermes, Jules, AGY, OpenCode, claude-ds) coordinates over the MCP bus but specialist roles are hand-written per lane. Most direct fits: the marketing team (37 agents) for the AI Marketing OS, and the engineering/QA loop for the Sports repo's review gates. Creative angles: cherry-pick, don't adopt wholesale — pull marketing and QA files as starting drafts for existing custom skills, adjudicated line-by-line; the orchestrator's 3-tries-then-escalate loop is the anti-drift law as code, a concrete pattern for the fleet bus task lifecycle; A/B one division in a claude-ds session against the hand-rolled lane on the same brief.
- **Risks:** These are prompts (caption itself warns): quality bounded by the input brief; files must be read before reliance; 165K stars measure popularity, not correctness; community project, not Anthropic's; installing all 283 wholesale would clutter every seat's context — division-scoped installs only; Claude Code seats need a paid plan or API billing.
- **Verdict:** TEST — install only the marketing division into a sandbox claude-ds session, run one real AI-Marketing-OS brief, grade against the incumbent lane.

### AM-19 — @repopicks — neuron-explorer — LEARN-ONLY
- **What it is:** A steering-first interpretability tool for a small open LLM (Qwen3.5-4B): pushes individual MLP neurons, keeps every rollout, results browsable in a local web explorer; a sweep mode runs on a GPU pod with a steering fork of vLLM; a chat panel can run a Claude CLI inside the explorer via MCP. The reel's voiceover oversells it as a fix for broken developer workflows; the repo itself is a research instrument.
- **Where:** https://github.com/lyramakesmusic/neuron-explorer (verified: 1,764★, 215 forks, MIT, pushed 2026-10-28, created 2026-10-21; caption's 1,218★ count is stale). Post: https://www.instagram.com/p/De_3H65gDSN/
- **How to use:** Per README: clone, `uv run python explorer/server.py` serves the explorer on localhost:8791; model loads locally on MPS/CUDA/CPU; sweep commands take neuron selections and control counts as flags.
- **System:** Model-routing/research stack as an interpretability specimen — his free-first stack routes among small open models (DeepSeek Flash, free OpenRouter models, Qwen-class locals); neuron steering is the layer below prompting. Creative angles: the explorer's shape (run sweep, keep every rollout, browse outcomes) mirrors engine backtest experiment-logging discipline; steering vs prompt-level self-improvement are two ends of one control spectrum.
- **Risks:** Ten days old; single-model scope; sweep mode wants a CUDA pod (cost); interpretability results are exploratory, not production levers; the reel's workflow framing is marketing.
- **Verdict:** LEARN-ONLY — catalog into the research corpus with the repo pinned.

### AM-20 — @git.radar — claude-howto — LEARN-ONLY
- **What it is:** A visual, example-driven guide to Claude Code pitched as the antidote to a 500-page manual: 10 tutorial modules from basics to advanced agents, copy-paste templates, diagrams, a guided learning path, self-assessment quizzes. The only selected post of the day whose caption carried the URL directly.

- **How to use:** Per README: start from the "Get Started in 15 Minutes" path, then work modules in order; templates are copy-paste into real projects. Documentation only — no install, no runtime.
- **System:** Agent fleet infrastructure as the training layer — AM-10 gave the 20-part map; this is the course that teaches each part. Any new Claude seat (or Garrett himself, climbing the funnel) onboards faster from it than from official reference docs. Creative angles: diff its module list against AM-10's 20 parts for curriculum gaps; its quizzes are a ready-made competence check for a new seat's operator docs; its success (46K★ for a how-to) is funnel-doctrine evidence — teaching the tool is a bigger market than another tool, relevant to Kit's education-adjacent offers.
- **Risks:** Third-party guide — Claude Code changes fast, individual pages can lag (last push Oct 29 helps); popularity is not accuracy; no executable risk.
- **Verdict:** LEARN-ONLY — bookmark as the fleet's Claude Code onboarding reference.

### AM relevant ad (not counted) — Aikido Device Protection — WATCH
Feed position 32 (ad, no permalink), feed-level data only: device protection that checks every npm package and browser extension and blocks malware before it runs, framed around recent supply-chain attacks. Genuinely relevant — on Oct 30 the sweep recorded a real supply-chain backdoor in a Claude Code VS Code extension (~5,000 installs, one-day window), and Garrett's fleet installs packages constantly. Aikido is a real security vendor (aikido.dev; not independently verified this run beyond the ad). **Verdict: WATCH** — install-time package screening on fleet machines is worth one evaluation pass alongside the own-systems audit kit assembled Oct 29–30; nothing clicked or claimed. Other ads noted without action: Granola (pos 22 — Garrett already uses it), Runway Ads (pos 52 — variant generation, Content OS-adjacent), ElevenLabs ElevenAgents (pos 80), Reliant Energy $200 bill-credit (pos 42, Texas electricity, household).

### AM synthesis (condensed)
- A free Kit+fleet production stack assembled from one feed: /awwwards (AM-14) builds the page, its companion /jury grades it, agency-agents (AM-18) supplies brief-writing specialists, claude-howto (AM-20) + the 20-part map (AM-10) train the operators. One pipeline: brief → build → screenshot-QA → iterate. Every piece MIT and verified live.
- The wrapper stack completed itself: AM-16's 703 prompts (raw material) + AM-15's Bang Motion (execution skill) + Oct 30's mg-styles-15 (style library) — prompt → style → render, all free, all MIT, all fenced to wrappers/cards/explainers by the standing video rule.
- Star counts in captions were stale in both directions (claude-howto +3.4K, neuron-explorer +546, agency-agents accurate) — primary-source checks remain the only reliable count. Two repo names were mis-transcribed by one provider route each (hyphenated opus repo 404s; "juliosz" 404s) — cross-checking caption vs stored description vs API caught both.
- Claude Code is the feed's default substrate (7 of 20 selected posts) — the feed is effectively shopping Garrett's own stack back to him, making adjudication the scarce skill, not discovery.
- Proposed test order: (1) /awwwards on one real Kit brief. (2) agency-agents marketing division in a sandbox claude-ds seat vs incumbent lane. (3) Bang Motion + one AM-16 prompt for a GSE stat-card opener. (4) python-training notebooks as a fleet math-lane exercise. (5) Win11Debloat on a snapshotted non-critical Windows profile.

## PM PART 2 (PM-FEED) DOSSIERS (20 — the actual evening feed)

### PMF-01 — @gittrend.io — Meldwork — TEST (design-level)
- **What it is:** A local-first, macOS multi-agent workspace: you pick the agents you already run, set goal/context/permissions, and its Auto Discussion mode has them propose approaches, request peer contributions, and check each other's deliverables, with scoped permissions, evidence-aware runs, and human-in-the-loop decisions. The narration cites a five-agent run completing 19 native calls (the project's own example, UNVERIFIED).
- **Where:** https://github.com/Ryder-Sun/Meldwork (verified: 1,280★, 80 forks, Apache-2.0, pushed 2026-10-30, created 2026-07-29; current release V1.0.8 per README). Post: https://www.instagram.com/reel/DfKrQOvFV8D/
- **How to use:** Per README: download the Apple-silicon macOS .dmg from Releases, choose participating agent tools, scope goal, working directory, context, attachments, skills, knowledge sources, permissions; workspace writes are opt-in; collaboration history and deliverables stay local. **Platform requirement: macOS (Apple silicon)** — Garrett's fleet seats are this Linux VM and a Windows box, so there is no native seat today.
- **System:** Agent fleet infrastructure, as a pattern first and a product second — Garrett already built this shape (fleet MCP bus, live at 127.0.0.1:4311, plus the Git agent-bus archive). Transferable assets: its scoped-permissions model (per-task, per-agent, opt-in workspace writes — the grant shape the bus's token system could grow), evidence-aware run records, and the peer-check discussion loop (propose → contribute → verify) for bus task lifecycle design. It does not replace the bus. Its "orchestrate goals, not tasks" framing is the anti-drift law in product language. If a Mac ever joins the fleet, this is the first coordination UI to try, with the bus remaining the record.
- **Risks:** Young (created July 2026), single-vendor, macOS-only; 1,280★ is early traction, not proof; local-first means its history doesn't sync to the bus without glue work.
- **Verdict:** TEST (design-level) — no install possible on current hardware; test = read its architecture doc against the fleet bus's task/permission model and lift what's better. WATCH for a Mac seat.

### PMF-17 — @gittrend.io — Aleph (geodata-for-LLMs) — TEST ⭐
- **What it is:** Draw a rectangle or polygon on a map, pick sources (Street View, satellite, OSM, terrain, 3D mesh), and the tool captures everything inside the area — buildings, elevation, HD satellite imagery, Street View photos, textured 3D models — or query a single point from the CLI (`alephgeo`), built explicitly to feed physical-world context to LLMs.
- **Where:** https://github.com/Belluxx/Aleph (verified: 592★, 57 forks, MIT, pushed 2026-10-30, created 2026-09-19; description: "Give satellite imagery, terrain data, buildings, streetview photos to your LLM"). Note the owner spelling: the narration's "Bellux" 404s; the live repo is Belluxx (double-x) — the same one-letter transcription trap the AM run caught twice. Post: https://www.instagram.com/reel/DfLRpkvlRMS/
- **How to use:** Per README: Python 3.11+; dependencies only Pillow, numpy, truststore; `git clone`, venv, `pip install .`, then `alephgeo --help`; `alephgeo dashboard -o captures` opens a local dashboard at 127.0.0.1:8100 to draw the area, choose sources, preview time/cost, and capture; captures save locally with per-layer toggles and a 3D mesh viewer. No API keys required.
- **System:** Three real seats: (a) Kit/client work — a one-command capture of a client's actual location (satellite + Street View + 3D) is instant, free raw material for a prospect preview page; pairs with AM-14: Aleph captures the real place, /awwwards builds the page around it — a Kit preview showing the prospect their own street in 3D is a different sales object than a template. (b) Place/venue research for GSE-adjacent local work (stadium surroundings, Houston places) where an LLM seat needs physical context instead of guessing — e.g., feed a stadium-district capture to a model seat as grounding for venue/weather/surface context notes. (c) Research corpus — the cleanest example yet of an "LLM physical-world context" tool: small, MIT, honest about its limits. The point-query CLI is a cheap geodata primitive for future location-aware agent tasks (travel, signage siting) without a Google Cloud account.
- **Risks (stated in its own README, decisive):** Street View, satellite, and 3D-mesh layers are Google data and downloading them goes against Google's terms; it relies on undocumented APIs that may break or rate-limit; OSM layers are ODbL (attribution + share-alike on modified databases); terrain is AWS Terrain Tiles (open, credit required). So: OSM/terrain layers are clean for reuse; the Google layers are look-but-don't-ship. Young repo (Sept 2026), single maintainer.
- **Verdict:** TEST — clone on this VM, capture one Houston location using OSM + terrain layers only, check output quality and the point-query CLI's usefulness for an LLM seat. Cheapest real capability gain in either PM part.

# PART 7 — YOUTUBE

**Lane state:** no YouTube sweep delivery exists for Oct 31 (the lane has been off since the Oct 21 kill order; output folders stop at 2026-10-16 — evidence in Part 8, item 0). **The YouTube content in this pack is the video Garrett supplied at 03:23 CT on Nov 1, extracted in full below.**

**Source:** https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Pm0W7rZ9_fM — Ship It Nation; hosts Tyler Tamboline (ToeTagginTambo) and Alex Blickle (Blick Analytics). Runtime 1:45:39. Supplied by Garrett 2026-11-01 03:23 CT for this pack. FULL transcript read (auto-captions; names normalized where phonetic, one unresolvable name in the 58:55 chapter flagged, not invented). "Week 8" in the video = the Nov 1–2 slate (same label offset as the freeze). All claims below are the hosts', attributed Tambo vs Alex where identifiable — host opinion, not GSE-verified.

**Why it matters to this slate (Motif note):** the flagship DraftKings cash lineups carry Jonah Coleman at RB and the Jets DST; the freeze carries Jake Ferguson OVER 14.5 receiving yards as a PUBLISH prop. This video speaks directly to all three: Coleman ownership ~15% per Alex (20–25% ceiling), Broncos DST — not Jets — as the popular Coleman pairing (37% conditional), and Alex's "negative interest" in Ferguson (snap share leaking to Spann-Ford). The hosts' Geno Smith stack segment (1:21:18) builds Geno with Garrett Wilson + Omar Cooper Jr. and Bowers as the bring-back — consistent with Garrett's ruling that Geno Smith is the Jets' QB.

## Chapter-by-chapter extraction

### 00:00 INTRO
- Tambo introduces himself and Alex Blickle for the Week 8 NFL DFS edition: break down the main slate, "the chalk build, how to leverage the chalk build, focusing on it from a single entry perspective for these large field GPPs," plus possibly some one-lineup discussion.
- Tambo: "it might be the first time we've ever both picked up Mega Millie tickets at the same time." Notes the upcoming $1,500 mid-season main event.
- Alex: Week 7 was "really fun," Week 8 is fun "for a lot of the same reasons"; last week was "the best I've played an NFL slate probably in like a year and a half." Recognizing a unique slate is an advantage: "when there's a unique slate… we're a little bit more equipped to kind of figure out how to attack that."

### 02:07 WEEK 7 TAKEAWAYS AND WINNING LINEUP CONSTRUCTION
- Tambo: the week is "a lot more about the takeaways" than the result; jokes "Tambo always wins in week seven," says he "almost won the Millie last week" and "had a real sweat down the stretch."
- Revisit of the Deshaun Watson vs. Cam Ward / Cleveland discussion. Alex: "we like made it pretty clear we don't think this is going to be some like explosive game, it's just what it allows us to do with the rest of the lineup."
- Alex: even without an outlier Watson outcome, Tambo "still almost won a million dollars," and "you were the highest scoring lineup without [Tony] Pollard." Tambo did not have Pollard; a long reception stopped at the 1-yard line was another way it could have hit more.
- Alex's Week 7: "played four lineups last week. Three of them cashed. Uh, one was a top 10% outcome, but my highest scoring lineup was a Cam Ward stack." "Cam Ward was atrocious. Uh, but it allowed me to fit a lot of stuff that I really liked in addition to Brock Bowers, who I played I think in three of the four, maybe even four of the four." That lineup also had Braylon Allen, Bucky Irving and Tee Higgins; "it was the Cam Ward sack [stack] that allowed me to fit that."
- Alex's three potential Week 7 Leverage Lanes were Bowers (chosen; "didn't do that well"), the Cleveland game stack, and "the Josh Jacobs pivot to kind of fade that Green Bay game." The other two "worked out really well" and were "the little bit more contrary, more off-the-wall type plays." He will "overcorrect if anything this week"; teases: "my leverage lane is going to be the most off-the-wall leverage lane I've ever given out and probably will ever give out this season." Tambo was already on Alex's Week 8 idea before hearing it in Discord; props made it "even more interesting."
- Tambo shows his Millie lineup via dfsos.com (contest vault + FanDuel data): built around Watson, a player captioned "Conpsion," and Fannin; no Pollard. In hindsight he wishes he had Pollard and Kraft opposite Amon-Ra St. Brown: "I would have won everything."
- Tambo: the Bucky Irving + Buccaneers defense pairing "didn't get talked about enough." Once you do that, you are most likely not playing Coker; Higgins at "5%" was an alternative in Coker's place. If you are on Buccaneers D and not the popular Titans D, "why not use the stack against them?" Framed as his "penthouse process": one move opens the next. "There was champagne getting sprayed on me… for fourth place."
- Tambo: that pairing also leveraged Coker and Hubbard "a little bit," and the Ted Hurst chalk: "Ted Hurst ended up like 40% in some single entries in high stakes stuff. I don't know what he was in in this Millie contest but probably like 25-30 would be my guess." Game script: Bucs defense doing well, Bucky getting "a ton of volume," leaving "not a lot of passing volume to go around for Hurst": "Hurst gets the zero. Like, it literally couldn't have been any better for that pairing."
- Alex: RB + defense pairings and mini-stacks are sometimes overused ("I'm clicking two players from the same game… Therefore, I must have a lot of added correlation"). Tambo's lineup was better: "there actually is a lot of correlation… a very clear and coherent story about not only how your guys do well, but how their success like actually leverages a lot of the chalk that you're not using."
- Tambo: for single-entry GPP players the focus should not be just "getting the most off-the-wall play." What Alex's play allows around it is the point: "you can now plug in almost anything you want around that."

### 09:42 CONTEST SELECTION AND SATELLITE STRATEGY
- Tambo on contest selection: do not treat a lineup as Millie OR satellite; play it in both: "you're playing it in the millie hoping to get that sweat and when it comes up just short, it's not ideal, but it's still 25,000… it's nice to just boat race the king of the desert field and get that ticket. So, it's like I'm playing it in both places." Think about that "depending on what you can afford."
- Tambo on Thursday Showdown: people criticize a defense captain, but Buccaneers vs Carolina "just helped me a lot and won me a bunch of money," so he tried one lineup with Packers D as captain: 5-1 build, a running back on the other side, played for a low-scoring game: "we kind of got 27-19. It wasn't like over the 46 and a half." Had Kraft, Jacobs, Hubbard and Love, Packers D captain. Result: "I never won this many tickets in one run with a lineup. I got two 444s, five main events for Sunday." On screen: "340 bucks to a 4444 mega ticket, 1,500 bucks cash." A commenter said the big GPP Showdown paid 3K; Tambo's reply: contest selection still paid — "you put it where it actually could matter and pay off the most. And it most certainly did."
- Alex on Showdown: "I'm at like 33% top 10% outcomes in Showdown and something like 8% top 1% so far this year." Explanation: "with Showdown, it's so easy to just tell like one coherent story about how the game plays out in an unexpected way… Main Slate, like you have to tell a coherent story about how eight games or 10 games combined to play out in an unexpected way, but not too unexpected." Adds: "we're playing the game of DFS so much more than we're playing fantasy football."
- Tambo jokes Alex wanted the Brock Bowers Leverage Lane and he will let Alex claim it.
- On the "big game" last week: Tambo: "last week the big game sort of busted again." The Millie-winning lineup got there with Goff; Tambo's leverage was "golf [Goff] japorta [LaPorta] everything," but a Goff / Jameson Williams / Amon-Ra lineup passed him — "three of the four guys that I was on… that ended up actually beating me for the Millie Maker win" — even though "the game really [didn't] go off crazy" and "you didn't need like Gibbs." The other Millie (the $555) had a second-place lineup that "lost by four points and had Gibbs." Tambo: "Can you imagine like going in the afternoon feeling like, 'Oh man, I got the highest Gibbs. I'm right in the mix of everything.'"
- Alex on Week 7 structure: "we had that like really really cheap wide receiver chalk that allowed people to play the expensive game stack in kind of whatever way they wanted." It was "hard to get to Gibbs" and "hard to get to Aman Ross Brown [Amon-Ra], too." Jameson Williams was "the cheapest piece of that game" and the best piece after the top one: "the guy who's really going to speed it up. So, he had the really big game." Alex's Jordan Love stack (one of four lineups) had Watson, Golden and Jameson Williams: "I got the huge game from Williams but didn't get it from the Green Bay guys." His three "really inversely correlated" lineups "ended up in the same spot instead of one of them really separating."
- Tambo on the $150 warm-up event: "Gre" had 213, "about five points back," but had Jahmyr Gibbs, while the leader was probably happy at the end but "where were those Puka points when he needed them." Blake Corum "just keeps going off"; a big touchdown callback hurt because it would have been "34 or 35 to 6" and probably shut things down; instead it became an Ashton Jeanty touchdown through the air, and the $20 Millie Maker winner had Jeanty in the same stack with Jared Goff and others. Corum "actually had two touchdowns called back and then also scored one… That could have been enormous for him."
- Both hosts missed on Bowers; Tambo: "the logic was sound" and "I'm going to go right back to it this week… in a lot of places," because Bowers was "one of the main ways that we were fitting" the Watson stack.

### 17:23 WEEK 8 SLATE OVERVIEW
- Tambo: the biggest difference is that Arizona–Dallas and Buffalo–Baltimore are "both going on at the same time. It does seem like people want way more action on one than the other and that is the Arizona Dallas." On Baltimore: "we got good news that Lamar's likely to play," coaches say "we hope or we think we'll have him out there," he has been practicing, Tambo expects him, with the total up. But reports that he "can't, you know, plant his foot exactly" scare people away. Zay Flowers "gets love for sure." On the other side, Kincaid is "still questionable, likely to go," Moore is "still questionable," people "never think they can get Josh Allen," so they discuss "playing him naked, but he's more expensive."
- Tambo: after those games, "there is a ton of big, you know, big enough 46, 45, 45" totals, "a lot of very stackable offenses with tighter target trees."
- Alex: "the really unique thing about this week is it's the first week of the season where I think the luxury payups are almost universally at wide receiver and not running back." The field will try to get to Bijan Robinson "a little bit" and to Gibbs, but "I think the field is probably overestimating Gibbs ownership at this point in the week." "I think Javvante will probably be the most popular like quote-unquote big spend at running back, but I think most of the running back ownership is going to be more mid-tier type stuff." People will try to get to "Lamb, Chase, Flowers, probably the big three. Higgins has a chance as well. Uh, Puka for sure, Jefferson, London, Pickkins will get some. Parker Washington's going to get some." Conclusion: "this is the first week where people are saving elsewhere to get to wide receiver spends instead of to get to running back spends," and "that kind of changes things… and it sets the table for… what my leverage lane is going to be at the end."
- Tambo: "I agree with all of that 100%," and "I think Chase Brown will be the highest owned running back." Javonte Williams is "definitely steaming up." On QB + RB: "It's if people steam up Javvante. I feel like that's more in lineups that they're not on Dak. And then I wonder if the Dak high ownership that everyone's seeing right now actually comes back a little bit and tightens up." Right now "we're just seeing Dak pull away a little bit," then the guys after him. Joe Burrow "could steam up a little. He's 700 bucks more. We already know Chase and Higgins are both popping. If people want to leverage Chase Brown some, you can play them with Chase Brown." On the Titans side, "no Tate and no Spears," so it is unclear if people go back to Pollard "after that amazing game." Tambo: "I just don't see both Dak and Javvante being super high-owned. I feel like it's more Javvante. He fits every build… you can put Chase Brown, Javvante in any stack this week right away, no problem."
- Alex: "I do think we'll see plenty of both" (Dak and Javonte) and "plenty of uh CD Lamb as well." One path to Dak not ending as high-owned as expected is "if a lot of that ownership just shifts to Jacobe Brassette [Jacoby Brissett] and people play the game through him instead," which is "very possible," but "it's only a $600 difference, which I think is probably going to keep [Brissett] in check and allow Dak to get up there in ownership."
- Tambo: early rosters he can already see are "Chase Brown, Javvante Williams, and Tooten [Tuten] in your two running back spots and your flex." That wraps around Brissett, Bourne, and Michael Wilson, "getting talked up again now because it's just affordable to make all that fit." Last week people built stars-and-scrubs because of the 3K Hurst; this week people are down on everyone that pops up, e.g., Jonah Coleman: "No, I don't know about that. Like people will get there… They're not in love with that. There's so many other good running backs. Do you need him?" He also cites a player captioned "Juell Robinson, he just catches the ball and falls on it. Like why would I ever play him at 4200?" and says Higgins is "picking up steam," with "all the mid-range guys that are going to just be filled in" and "ways that those lineups can just get stomped on."

### 23:38 BUILDING THE CHALK LINEUP
- Alex: "Raandre Stevenson at 5600 [is] a really popular pick. uh he's actually who I have second in uh in single entry and high stakes ownership and getting over 30% in high stakes." Evidence the field agrees "the goal of this slate is largely to get to as many of those stud wide receivers that have the slate breaking upside as possible. and both Chase Brown and Reandre Stevenson are like a really easy foundation to help do that as is Bashalt Tunin [Tuten]."
- Alex: Tuten is "probably… what's going to keep Javvante Williams in check." He has Williams as "the third most popular running back at the moment. Um but only slightly over 20%," because people "actually want to spend money on elsewhere and want to save money at running back this week more so than like pretty much any slate we've seen so far this season. And we have the pieces to actually do that in Brown, Stevenson, and Tun [Tuten]."
- Tambo: in the Tuten version he sees Hunter Henry at TE; swapping Tuten to Rhamondre Stevenson flips Henry to Brenton Strange. "Tuton dropping 300 and Hunter Henry dropping 300 to flip it gives you 600… gives you [Brissett] to Dak." Ownership numbers "can even be off slightly, but it's all the game theory that comes with that of like, okay, if everyone's doing this, how can I do it where it's not silly, stupid… but do it in an intelligent way that can also give you… separation." A low-owned Leverage Lane play is not "just jamming eight more plays like that"; it is "your unlock," "probably triple leverage… It's going to leverage something. It's going to leverage roster construction."
- Chalk build start: "let's go Chase Brown," then Tuten. On Henry vs. Strange, Tambo says ownership is "so close that you know it's hard to tell," with a setup of Mason Taylor having "crushed this team, so therefore Hunter Henry's got to crush this team," plus uncertainty with Patriots running backs, and more people wanting the home running back in Tuten and ending up with more Henry.
- Alex: "I have more Stevenson ownership projected than [Tuten] ownership, but that could change. I'm pretty confident that Henry is going to be higher than Strange. I only have Strange at like 15%. I have Henry over 20% in single entries." Henry is the guy "people really go to," partly because of the Miami matchup and partly because "there's no AJ Brown, there's no Matt Collins, there's… no Romeo Doss [as captioned]… a lot of passing weapons out for New England and people want a piece of that offense kind of no matter what. And I think Henry's the clear and obvious answer," whereas receivers "have really nice ceilings for their prices like they also have really low floors. Henry has a really nice floor." Alex: "if in my update tonight I end up with Henry over 30%, like I'm not going to be surprised. He's a very clear and obvious play as a salary saving option this week."
- Tambo's showcased (not necessarily exact) chalk version: "a balanced piece of everything": "you've got Burrow Brown Higgins [Ayomanor] on the other side just because he's cheap obviously [Wan'Dale] could be popular at 4200 as well." The one-off in the Baltimore–Buffalo game is most likely Zay Flowers; the build also has Jacksonville, Dallas covered, and Denver because people say, "Hey, if I'm going to play the cheap defense, let's get the one at home against Mahomes. Some of those guys are a bit banged up even though they're coming back." "A lot of people will have some variation" of it, and "plugging Dak into it is not that hard. We actually pick up $700."

### 30:07 PREDICTING OWNERSHIP AND THE FIELD'S BUILDS
- Alex on method: "we're not using an optimizer to determine what the field is going to do. We're using like years of study of what the field does. That's how I create my ownership projections. It's how I create my field lineups for the contest sims." Not tool-dependent; "anybody who plays DFS over time, you just kind of get a sense of this." Building the most popular build is about "what are the easy clicks? What are the clicks that people are going to make where they feel like… there's not a single thing about that click that's giving them pause… It's clear and obvious to them that, hey, what I'm doing right now is not a mistake. And that's… what the chalk build ends up looking like."
- Tambo: "there's definitely an art to the science." At Ship It Nation simulations are run, but "when it says optimal, that's optimal lineup… that's them hitting the optimal lineup, not just them as an optimal play." It's about "how you're putting the pieces together," plus the art of saying "I think [the field] would do this and if they do this, then that would probably mean they just land here." The showcased build is a "pretty good lineup… a lot of the best plays. That's why you see there's heavy ownership associated with all of them and position dependent like 11% quarterback is still pretty high when most quarterbacks sit at five or 6% or less." After a 3K guy like Hurst last week, "everyone's sort of filling it out the balanced version the rest of the way." He notes: "You don't see Gibbs, you don't see Robinson, you don't see McBride, you don't see uh Jamar Chase, you don't see Lamb in this lineup, you don't see Almond [Amon-Ra]. I could keep going on any expensive defenses." The problem is "not only are you missing those expensive pieces, but you're missing the build around them and how it works together. That's what the Deshun Watson stuff worked out… last week because last week most people were on the studs, not the mid-range. Opposite of this [week]."

### 33:01 JOE BURROW VS DAK PRESCOTT STACK CONSTRUCTION
- Alex: "Burrow projects under 10%. So, I think a lot of people watching might be like, well, why do you have Burrow in here? Well, Burrow is an example like one of those easy clicks that just kind of naturally happens based on what else is in the lineup. So, T. Higgins, Chase Brown, both very naturally in this [chalk] build. [Ayomanor] very naturally in this [chalk] build is like the one punt that people seem really intrigued by and so if you already have like two Bengals and a Titan Burrow makes a ton of sense."
- A "one play removed from the chalk build" variation: Chase Brown to Higgins, Javonte down to Rhamondre, Henry to Strange. People might also leave Henry and not go to Strange since Tuten is already in the lineup; other cheap TEs include Cole Kmet [captioned "Kate Utten"], Mark Andrews, Hawkinson [Hockenson], or others: "there are plenty of cheap options this week."
- Alex: "I think the Steelers will be the popular pay up defense when people are paying up and this gets Javante back in while still covering the angles."
- Tambo testing a Burrow double with Ja'Marr Chase instead of Javonte: "I think it's probably doable if you just go cheap at uh at defense." Sorting by ownership, use the Broncos; "If we need 300 bucks, we'll go down to the Chargers. But this leaves you 600 bucks still to go wherever you want." Point: "it's not difficult to build a Cincinnati double this week because of [Ayomanor]. So the Cincinnati double I think is going to be used more often this week than it has so far this season." The Dallas double is harder because there is no super-cheap RB on the Tennessee side like Ayomanor. He hesitates to call the built lineup a chalk build because "there wasn't a single cowboy in it," so he "fixed it and put a cowboy in it," swapping Zay to Lamb and dropping Broncos to Chargers, with no Rams, so "you feel good about the Chargers at stone minimum." Result: "Burrow Higgins Chase [Ayomanor]" as a double stack with a runback, plus a "double Jacksonville but it's like a running back and a tight end. So… I don't think people will care about that too much."
- Same reasoning for New England: Tambo does not think it is crazy to see Rhamondre with Henry, because people "don't stack that game because they don't want to try and figure out Drake Maye and we're still waiting on AJ Brown news and then there's Kyle Williams and Efton Chisum and Deario Douglas and you name it… So, I think people just would rather say, 'Hey, give me some New England, but let's just do it this way and play Raandre and/or Henry.'"
- The flip is "the exact same… there's 300 bucks… different $5,600 guy." Tambo: "Chase Brown is going to be super popular no matter what. He's getting pushed off the board regardless."
- Alex on conditional ownership: "yet another example of conditional ownership. If you're kind of building something that's very chalky, then burrow actually might fit more naturally than DAC. But remember… most of the field isn't necessarily building from the chalk out the way we are." Many people start with "what is the stack that I want," and more start with "I want to use Dak Prescott this week and that's why Dak's going to end up higher," but if you build something really chalky and then go Burrow, "that's probably not getting you as low conditional ownership as you think… you're running into higher conditional ownership that way."
- For Dak, Alex: other QBs "just might be less correlated. So if you're going with Dak, it's probably more likely to be a single with just Lamb," or the reverse: Brissett with just McBride or just Wilson and then just Lamb as the bring-back, or "using Lamb and Javvante as the bring back." "A little bit harder to figure out the ways in which people are going to use Dak and there's a wider variety whereas that burrow build is like very clear and obvious in how it's going to happen because it's being played through some of the chalkiest pieces on the slate."
- Tambo: "I still agree with you but I definitely think that Dak and Joe Burrow are going to tighten up." Anytime you see "big projections big ownership big optimal whatever you see of like Chase Higgins and a value multiple value plays people tend to lean towards that," and Chase Brown ownership is "probably even higher than this especially in the high stakes." Some people get fed up hearing about Chase Brown and move off him, when "you could have just played him differently."
- Tambo on Javonte (captioned as the $7,400 player): with Chase Brown and Higgins, a version with Lamb and Javonte allows "more moves and different moves." He notes a player "a hundred bucks less than T. Higgins," and a hamstring worry: "it's a big… deal. But sometimes when you see something like this and people are like, 'Well, it's just [Tee] Higgins. I can't play Jamar Chase.' But you already got Chase Brown, too, which could slow the game down. It could be all him on the ground. Like sometimes it's better to just like plug in that guy and get somebody that's a lower owned piece of a stack that you're already on." "We'll see where Pickin's ownership actually comes in… sometimes it's more about just getting the secondary piece or the piece that actually makes the stack go versus just playing the chalk oneoff."
- Alex: that could also be "a Michael Wilson bring back there to help uh push Dak and that offense."
- Alex: "it's a lot easier to get to these you know pretty expensive wide receivers but not super expensive wide receivers," partly because "no wide receiver on this [slate is] priced anywhere close to Jir Gibbs uh or or even Bashawn Robinson [Bijan Robinson] other than Puka," and "we actually have the running backs this week to do it, which is something we haven't had a lot of this year." On some slates $6,100 was the pay-down option, saving money at RB "but now I'm giving up a ton of projection elsewhere." This week the cheap/mid-tier RBs are "actually probably the best values just from a pure projection standpoint anyway. And they help get to the wide receivers." Structural summary: "it's very very clear that the chalk build this week from a structural standpoint is mid-tier to cheap running back, pay up uh at wide receiver, save money at tight end."

### 49:02 JAHMYR GIBBS OWNERSHIP AND THE VIKINGS MATCHUP
- Tambo on a chat claim that Gibbs still gets jammed a lot: "I don't see how. And don't forget he failed people last week. You're going against uh Flores… Blitz and all this other stuff. Maybe he could get some checkdowns… but he still is almost $10,000. And we see that people are building balance. So, I think if you like Gibbs this week, you should be able to get, you know, 20 to 25% and see where it lands. If he gets steamed, if people still can't get away from him somehow, then, you know, you'll probably be around field, but if people stay off of him, you could be double the field at 25%. You never know."
- Alex: his baseline ownership is more accurate here because of conditional ownership. Last week, "while Gibbs did get to a decent overall ownership number, his conditional ownership outside of Green Bay Detroit game stacks was pretty low." People looking back at his ownership last time he was at a price like this see elevated ownership "because he was involved in the most popular game of the week. He's not involved in the most popular game of this week. And I think consequently, we're going to see his ownership come way, way down this week." Alex's numbers: "I have 14% MME, 13.5% high stakes, 13% single entry. There's a pretty decent chance I think that I'm going to end up in single digits for either high stakes or… single entry. Probably single entry will end up being the lowest and has the highest chance of him ending up single digits just because again like I think way more people are paying for wide receivers who are literally $2,000 cheaper than him."
- Alex: "this is one of the only weeks this year where… [wide receivers] have a ceiling that can compete with his. Most weeks Gibbs is in a tier of his own ceiling wise. I don't think that's really the case against this Minnesota defense. They do such a good job with the run blitzes, not just… all the blitzing. Their pressure scheme forces running backs to stay in." Recalls last year: "there was a game last year where Gibbs was essentially benched because they preferred Montgomery's blocking… to his. And… now Montgomery's not here, so like I'm not saying he's going to get benched. I also think his pass protection has been better this year even though it still is… not quite where they would like it to be. But… the matchup as a whole just really takes from the ceiling more than his floor." Combined with being "crazy price prohibitive at a position where there's more salary relief than we've gotten at… pretty much any slate this week or this year": in past slates at $9,500 you could save "$2 and a half to $3,000 with the cheaper end running backs that were really viable in the slate. This week you can save $4,000. People are going to take that."
- Tambo: a Detroit beat writer piece said Jahmyr Gibbs was on pass blocking "15 times in his first game against Minnesota last year," which the writer called crazy. He cites another item: "Brian Flores is crazy" showing his blitz graphs — "his [blitz rate] is the top right. Talk about the penthouse process. This man is in the penthouse at the very top right corner for blitzes. This he's just coming at you and figure it out or try to." People may say that could mean checkdowns for Gibbs; Tambo says you could see Amon-Ra and LaPorta "mixing it up over the middle," but "when you just catch the ball and fall on it the clock keeps running. So… it doesn't always mean great for you. It just means they could be moving the chains a little bit and then a bunch of runs to keep them off the field and back and forth. And if the other side doesn't pick up, it could make it so it's not as crazy as you think. You'll rack up some PPR points, but you want a little bit more than that sometime."

### 53:47 HOW BRIAN FLORES' DEFENSE CHANGES THE SLATE
- Tambo: "Is it worth nerding out for a second about what Flores is doing that's so unique?" Alex: "I think the chat loves that. Yeah, do it."
- Alex (crediting others' film work): basketball coaches used to say the offense's advantage "almost universally is that the offense can make the defense react to what the offense is doing. Flores is totally flipping that. Flores is making the offense react to what he's doing every single play."
- Alex's blitz-protection example, with the caveat "I can't prove that he's doing this, but it sure looks like he's doing it": with two guys lined up to blitz, pass protection often has the RB take one potential blitzer and the guard take the other. "Essentially the two guys start to rush and whoever realizes, oh, the guard is blocking me will abandon the rush and drop back into the coverage. And then the guy who has the one-on-one with the running back will continue to go. And so the guard ends up blocking nobody. The running back has to [block] the blitzer. Like it's just brilliant." Result: "they're getting like all these extravagant pressures only sending five. Sometimes they're sending four, but it's like you as an offense you can't know which four are coming and sometimes it's overloaded. It's just so so advanced."
- Alex: Flores is "brilliant in the way… he predicts how each offense individually. So, like he's not running the same plays over and over, but he's dropping those cover guys into… the exact areas of the field where he thinks that that specific offense is going to attack. It's really really cool."
- Alex on Goff: "there's also some pretty cool data on like how much Goff… has struggled with this against him in their last few meetings."
- Alex on a newer wrinkle (learned this week, attributed to a Philly beat writer): the change was made partly because of how Jalen Hurts picked them apart last week, but "sometime late last season, it seemed like they started to allow Harrison Smith to call audibles like right before the play." Against smarter veteran QBs like Goff, a past advantage vs Flores defenses was diagnosing "what's coming and… where the blitz is coming from [and] what type of coverage they're in… right before the snap he can change the play. Well, what Harrison Smith is doing is right before the snap, he's changing the play for Minnesota. And now the offense doesn't even have a chance to react with their own audibles ahead of the snap. Like, it's just so ahead of their time."
- Alex predicts: "in the next 2, three years, we're going to see way more defenses playing this way. But… it's not just that Flores has come up with this totally new way of doing it, but he's also executing it at a really high level and his players have now been playing this crazy aggressive, really complex style for long enough that… they're now comfortable in it," whereas a team just starting it would be asking, "Well, what am I supposed to do? How are we supposed to handle all this stuff at once?"
- Alex's DFS conclusion: "from a DFS perspective, it means I kind of want no part of anyone that's going up against them. And it also makes it harder to play Vikings because even in a game like this, I think offensively… they're not trying to say, 'Oh, we're playing Detroit. We need to really keep up and play a super aggressive style of offense.' I think they still are going to trust their defense."
- Tambo: chat user Burger said, "Vikes D for all the money. You sold them." Tambo notes the show covers NFL DFS and PGA DFS year-round and asks how many Minnesota followers there are.

### 1:01:54 RUNNING BACK PIVOTS WITH SEPARATING CEILINGS

His explanation: one point of projection is not just "he's going to get one point more than he otherwise would have in… every outcome. It's… in some outcomes, he just got you like 10 to 15 extra DraftKings points through receiving work that he otherwise never would have had with Spears or he stayed in for an extra drive when they… were trailing late because they didn't have Spears to get some work to. So… it's more meaningful than what that small projection boost can sometimes show. So maybe that's why he ended up a higher GPP score. But I think he was a pretty good GPP score all week. So if I did say something negative about him to Pat, I guess I'll have to apologize."

It will project well. You'll be able to make a case for whatever you want… But… there's different ways to go about the build. So, let's talk about these other game stacks."

### 1:10:24 BILLS VS RAVENS AND QUARTERBACK CORRELATION
- Tambo: after the 46-pointish totals, the Buffalo–Baltimore game, "at the big boy total that just people can't figure out how to stack it or if they do, we know they're kind of going to Lamar with maybe one or two again." He asks how many people are playing "Lamar, Zay, Lane [Likely], and Andrews and something different versus how many people are saying, 'Okay, I can get Zay in there, or I could go with Zay and Lane, or I can go with Zay and Andrews.' Like, I see that being the side where people would end up on."
- Alex introduces Jason's "naked quarterback lineup as… a lever" comment and imagines "an all knowing fantasy god Chrome extension… every time you click a player… there's like a bar that adjusts and that bar is your top 1% outcome likelihood." Starting blank, "you've got a 1% chance for a top 1% outcome. And now you click somebody and it adjusts a little bit… maybe it really likes Lamar Jackson this week and so it goes to 1.1%." It tells "your real… true probability of a top 1% outcome. Not… what the Sims are telling you, what a… perfect all knowing tool is telling you. You want to maximize that true top 1% outcome probability."
- Alex: a choice like "going naked or saying, you know what, I really like Lamar Jackson here. I really like Z Flowers, but I don't like anything else. I'm just going to skinny stack… it's going to bring that bar down. It's going to take you below 1%. You're now helping everybody else have a… better chance at a top 1% outcome because you're doing something that just makes it a lot harder for you." It can still hit sometimes: "if you go from 1% to [1.5%] as soon as you choose Lamar and Zay and then you drop down to [0.8%] because you didn't include any more correlation than that afterwards, it's like you still have a [0.8%] chance at a top 1% outcome."
- Alex: someone building that way will sometimes take down a contest with such a lineup. "Doesn't mean you should have done it. Doesn't even mean they should have done it unless they have some… perfect premonition about who's going to do exactly what… You're just making things harder on yourself that way."
- Alex on Baltimore–Buffalo specifically: "I don't really know how to approach it because I do think Z Flowers is a tremendous play. I do think both Josh Allen and Lamar Jackson in a vacuum are tremendous plays. It's just how do I build an actually good lineup with them that's well correlated? And I don't know the answer to that. I do think… the best answer is probably Lamar Lane [Likely] Z, but I also think… it might be a week where we can do some fun stuff where it's like it's Josh Allen single stacked and then two Baltimore guys is the bring back and you just hope that you get the multi-rushing touchdown game from Josh Allen. And the problem there is… even if I make that decision, I still don't have any clue which Buffalo guy I should be using with him."
- Tambo: "for me on those, especially with a good quarterback, I'm fine with a skinny and like a run back, but… I'm probably still trying to… if I land on the double and then the double also eats away at a chalk receiver that instead of just plugging in the one-off, I'm playing the guy that fits the stack. I prefer just to live and die by the double stack there." This reflects his season adjustment: Weeks 1–5 "much better than it's ever been," Week 6 "was horrible," Week 7 he always wins. It does "make my ticker go down a little bit, but remember, I'm working with a pretty good ticker over here the rest of the build. So… if I make that one decision, I know if I can make that or not, and I know what I'm accepting by doing it. That's the important part of DFS and understanding the game."
- Tambo's broader warning: when someone says a team is "very strong against the run, even if that's true, and I don't know that it fully is… it actually has no bearing on today's game." Matchup "obviouses and must bees or the always this way always and only can get you into a lot of trouble in DFS because it's not true, right? Anything can happen. Shout out to my guy Kevin Garnett. Anything is possible… We're not cutting and pasting productions… We're talking about builds and ways to leverage and ways to think about putting yourself in the position."
- Alex on granular matchups: Cincinnati is "number one in stuffed rate on run plays. They're 26th in open field yards per attempt. And so, what this says to me is like Pard has a very wide range of outcomes in his rushing this week where he might just get stuffed over and over and over again, especially because Cincinnati probably isn't going to be respecting the pass in any way. It might be… a Josh Jacobs efficiency-esque type performance from Pard. It also might be a case where most of the time he's getting stuffed or he's getting very little, but oh, he happened to have two 30 yard touchdowns or… one 30 yard touchdown and a 60-yard touchdown. And guess what? Those two runs just made him a slate breaker again." The range is "pretty wide," and "if he was 20%, 30% owned, something like that, then… I have no interest whatsoever in that type of range of outcomes for him. But if he's going to be really contrarian, and certainly if he's going to be… the most contrarian way to play a Cincinnati stack where those explosive runs are also going to really really help that Cincinnati stack, then it becomes viable." Agrees with a commenter: "I'm really not that interested in [Pollard], but if I were playing a Cincinnati stack, yes, like that's when you do [play him]."
- Tambo: "It's not 20% own[ership]… Nobody wants Tony Pard this week. He's probably going to be 10%. Nobody's playing this guy. What we're saying is… people are playing Joe Burrow and Chase Brown and Jamar Chase and T. Higgins and then they're plugging in [Ayomanor] or Robinson or whatever else it is. You can go to a Ridley if… assuming he plays. I think he's Q tagged but he's likely to play. You got him. You could go to Helm at tight end like he said." On building for an outcome: "When it doesn't happen, it goes in the garbage with the rest of them… even if you win on Sunday, it's likely that it was one lineup that came through for you. So, even somebody out there with 150, 149 go to the pasture to die. The one lineup shows up at the top."
- Tambo: "I don't care about Tony Pard the pick or the play. It's if I was building a Bengals stack and thinking of a way that could help it get there and get to a ceiling. That's what you'd be thinking about and then I wouldn't care about the matchup. I would care about the one time that the game goes this way." Same for simulations: "we sim this [slate] 10,000 times. This is what happened… But it's is it real? No, because there's… 9,999 you're not seeing. And it can be any of those as well. So people… go, I'm betting all these props because the simulation said this. Well, you got to think about the rest of the stuff, too."
- Alex, closing this game: "I agree with you that it is one where… the best route is to break the rule and… use a little bit less correlation than usual. But to me that means… I'm not really going to consider it for single entry, three entries, stuff like that. That's just something that… if I were… MMEing this week, you know, I'd be overweight on both and I'd play it that way and then I'd get all my correlation in… other builds."
